# The flows

What runs, step by step, for each event that matters to how LBTerm feels: every process, thread, lock, copy, allocation, wake and hop between them, from the event to the pixels on the screen. Each step says where it is, what runs it, how often, what it costs and whether it needs to run. CODEMAP.md says where the code is; this says what the code does each time, so a change to make LBTerm faster starts from knowing all of it rather than from trying values (CLAUDE.md, "Knowledge first").

How it's kept:

- A flow is traced from the code and from a run: the code for what runs and in what order, a profile or a stack sample (`lbterm/perf/sample.ps1`; PITFALLS 95 to 97 say how far to trust it) for what it costs and what the code doesn't show (the driver's, the OS's, a crate's). Each row says how it was found out.
- Whoever learns something about a step adds or corrects its row in the same commit as the work that needed it: a step nobody listed, a cost measured, a thread hop, a lock held.
- `Needs to run` is the reason the step exists, or `no` with what would do without it. A redesign starts from the `no` and `doubtful` rows, and when it lands their rows change with it.
- No paths of this PC: these go to the public repo.

| Flow | Traced |

## Pane output on a mux domain, to the frame that shows it

Every tab of Lachlan's lives on the mux domain (MUX.md), and 8 to 18 Claude Code sessions print into them all day, most out of view, so this is what LBTerm costs most of the time: from a pane's program writing to its console, through its conpty host, the mux server, the socket and the GUI, to the frame on the screen.

Traced on 2026-11-03 and 04 on main's code of that day (e7ba89150, with perf/fonts-core), in four segments, each from the code and one profiling run of its own ("The runs", below): 1, the program's write to the server's terminal model (ConPTY, the reader, the parser); 2, the server's main thread, from the notice to the bytes in each connection's socket; 3, the GUI, from those bytes to the window invalidated; 4, the frame, from the invalidate to the screen. Each hand-off between segments is a row of its own (18, 41 and 67). "Found out by" names the code read, a run (1 to 4) or the doc a number comes from; a cost said as estimated was worked out from the code, and the row says how. Where two segments' traces didn't agree, the row follows the code and "Where the traces disagree" says so.

### The processes and threads it crosses

- The pane's program (Claude Code on Node, a shell): its own process, writing to its console.
- `OpenConsole.exe`, the pane's conpty host: one a pane, a child of `lbterm-mux-server`, 5 threads (6 in one of run 1's). It takes each console call, keeps its screen buffer and writes VT down the output pipe.
- `lbterm-mux-server`:
  - Four unnamed threads a pane (three on this flow): `split_child`'s waiter (`mux/src/localpane.rs`, waiting on the program's exit, idle otherwise); the terminal's writer (term's `terminalstate/mod.rs`, `ThreadedWriter`, started with the terminal), blocked on its channel until the program's input comes (a key, a paste, a reply to a query and, since perf/mux-client, a pane's `writer()`), which it writes to the pty's pipe in order; the reader (`read_from_pane_pty`, started by `Mux::add_pane`), almost always blocked in ReadFile, woken by each ConPTY write, which it sends on over a loopback TCP pair; and the parser (`parse_buffered_data`, started by the reader 1 to 30 ms after it), almost always blocked in recv or asleep in its coalescing timer, which parses, performs under the pane's terminal lock, raises alerts and queues the notices. Tools tell them apart by stack or by start order (PITFALLS 100).
  - The main thread (`main`), promise's `SimpleExecutor`: one unbounded flume FIFO of boxed closures, drained by `loop { executor.tick() }`, parked in WaitOnAddress when idle (94.3 and 94.9% of its samples in runs 1 and 2). It runs the PaneOutput and alert tasks, `Mux::notify` and every connection's subscriber; each connection's dispatch loop as a task of its own (Pending, the push, `compute_changes` with its line and process reads, the encode with zstd, the socket's send and recv); every client PDU's task (keys, answers); and the cli's requests. It is the other taker of each pane's terminal lock.
  - The listener thread (`spawn_listener` → `LocalListener::run`): blocks in accept, sets each connection's send buffer and spawns its task on the main thread. Not on the push path.
  - `async-io`, async-io 2.6's reactor over polling 3.11's IOCP and AFD polls (about 1 ms a second in run 1), blocked in GetQueuedCompletionStatusEx: wakes a connection's task when its socket is readable (a client PDU) or writable again (the outbox was full). Reads and writes are tried directly on the main thread, so pushes need it only after the socket filled.
  - `lbterm-keep-socket` (the socket file every 3 s), `lbterm-jobs` and `lbterm-procs` are on other paths, though a push's cwd read can run on the main thread itself (row 27).
  - `lbterm-resize` (since perf/mux-client): one a pane while a window's resize is applied to it, started by the main thread and gone when the resize is (the resize flow, "A window's resize on a mux domain", row 7). It holds the pane's terminal lock through the whole rewrap (25 to 30 ms a pane of 50,000 lines on that branch's build), so that pane's parser (row 12) and a push the main thread works out for it (row 27) wait on the thread meanwhile; a key, poll or fetch for the pane waits in the resize book instead (`Resizes::after`) and doesn't hold the main thread.
- The kernel between them: the anonymous output pipe (NPFS), the parser's loopback TCP pair (AFD and TCP), `afunix.sys` for the server's socket, win32k for InvalidateRect and WM_PAINT, dxgkrnl for the submits and presents.
- `lbterm-gui`:
  - The mux client thread, unnamed (`Client::new`'s `thread::spawn`, running `smol::block_on(client_thread_async)` inside the reconnect loop). It waits, reads, decodes, counts, merges in the backlog and queues the GUI thread's takes; it encodes and writes the GUI's requests (keys, polls, GetLines); it sleeps while the backlog is held at LIMIT. 1,453 ms of CPU in 10 s in run 3, 14.5% of a core.
  - `async-io`, async-io 2.6's own reactor thread (`driver.rs`, `main_loop`). Which of the two holds async-io's reactor isn't known, and it changes hands. The client thread takes it in `block_on` when it has nothing to do, and lets it go whenever `react` returns with nothing for the client thread itself after it has held it over 500 µs (every async-io timer the GUI thread arms interrupts it with `Reactor::notify`, so after a wait that's every time), unparking the `async-io` thread. The `async-io` thread also takes it by itself: while any `block_on` runs (the client thread's always does) it wakes on a backoff of 50 µs to 10 ms, `try_lock`s the reactor when its tick hasn't moved since its last look, and after 10 such sleeps with no tick (about 20 ms of quiet) blocks on `lock()`, taking the reactor the moment the client thread next lets it go, which it does each time its own socket wakes it. While the `async-io` thread holds it, the client thread's `try_lock` fails and it parks, so each socket event reaches it as GetQueuedCompletionStatusEx on the `async-io` thread and then an unpark, a hop between two threads; and the GUI thread's async-io timers (the backlog's GAP, the budget's and the slow log's wakers) fire on whichever thread holds it. CPU time can't say which: `react(None)` is a blocking wait either way. Run 3 had the `async-io` thread under 15.6 ms of CPU in 10 s, run 4 47 ms in 11.3 s, which fits the reactor changing hands between runs ("Not traced yet").
  - The GUI thread (`main`): woken by SetEvent on the spawn queue's event out of MsgWaitForMultipleObjects. It applies the pushes, runs each notice's two task hops a window, the invalidate, the polls' and fetches' tasks and the whole frame, the NVIDIA user-mode driver's work for its submits and presents included (no driver thread was busy in run 4). 2,109 ms of CPU in 10 s in run 3, 21%.
  - `lbterm-state`, test windows only: the state file after every frame.
- The GPU, and `dwm.exe`, which composes the window's visual tree at each vblank of a screen with a change.

### 1. The program's output, to the server's terminal model

| # | Step | Where | Process, thread | Runs | Costs | Needs to run | Found out by |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | The program writes to its console: a WriteFile or WriteConsoleW a write, which the console driver (condrv) hands to the pane's conpty host as a message | the pane's program; Windows' console driver (not LBTerm's code) | program, the thread that writes | each console write. Real Claude Code (2.1.317, fullscreen, run 5) writes each frame whole in one console write, wrapped in synchronized output (CSI ?2026h at its start, CSI ?2026l at its end), having asked DECRQM 2026 twice as it started (row 9): all 251 of run 5's frames, and while only its spinner went its process wrote 45 times and 3,116 B for 45 frames, the same bytes the conpty host passed on and the reader read. A frame of the spinner alone (its glyph and timer) is 65 B at the median (89 p90, 113 most), about 5.5 a second, 118 ms apart at the median; while an answer streams it draws no text, only the spinner and a token count, 76 B (118, 144) about 7.7 a second, then the whole answer in one frame when it ends (10,524 B for 160 lines, 67 rows rewritten); a turn of tool calls about 14 frames a second, one a call moving the conversation up 4 or 5 rows (DECSTBM, SU and the rows that join it, 505 to 749 B: row 13) and the rest a row in place. Outside the brackets, each in a write of its own: its user var and title at its start, its DECRQM queries, and OSC 7501 program status at each turn's start and end; no title while it works (MUX.md saw one every 960 ms from an earlier version). mux-load's pwsh stand-ins write in .NET's console chunks of 256 characters (`Console.Out`'s StreamWriter), about 2.5 writes a frame of a Claude-like tab and 10 for each 2,440-byte string of the burst tab, and bracket nothing (PITFALLS 127) | the program's own CPU and a kernel round trip a call. Claude Code's process took 480 ms of CPU in 8.3 s of spinner (5.8% of a core) and 1,862 ms in 5.7 s of tool calls (run 5, its process cycles at the nominal clock) | yes: it is the output | run 5 (the recording, the processes' I/O counters), code (the stand-ins) |
| 2 | The conpty host takes each console call on its I/O thread, runs its own VT state machine to keep its screen buffer, cursor, modes and title in step (for programs that read the console back), and writes VT out to the output pipe. ConPTY 1.22 and later pass the program's VT through rather than render the buffer at a frame rate (Microsoft's ConPTY notes, its source not read here). Run 1 agrees: one pipe write a console call, and bytes out equal to bytes in (the burst tab 643 writes a second of 248 B, 155.7 KB/s, its 65 strings a second of 2,440 B; a Claude-like tab 15.4 KB/s, its 962-byte frames about 16 a second). Run 5 agrees for real Claude Code and shows what passes: one pipe write a console call (45 for its 45 spinner frames, 45 condrv messages), bytes out equal to bytes in, and DECSET, DECRST and DECRQM 2026 passed on as written (the probe tab's CSI ?2026$p and its two frames read exactly as its pwsh wrote them, the frame written in two calls 62.6 ms apart as two reads). Its own output at a pane's start: CSI 1t, then about 0.2 ms later CSI 6n, CSI c, CSI ?1004h and CSI ?9001h (INHERIT_CURSOR's position query, DA, focus events, win32-input-mode), then CSI 1;1H; and CSI 6n and CSI 1;1H again after each resize | `OpenConsole.exe`, the bundled ConPTY 1.25.2609.30003 (`assets/windows/conhost`), chosen by `pty/src/win/pseudocon.rs`, `load_conpty` | conpty host, one a pane, 5 threads (6 in one), a child of `lbterm-mux-server` | each console call: 40.3 to 40.5 writes a second for a Claude-like tab, 643 for the burst tab, 6.4 for the echo tab (one a key); real Claude Code 5.5 a second with the spinner alone, 7.6 while an answer streams, 14.3 in a turn of tool calls (run 5) | run 1, its process cycles over 10.06 s at the nominal clock: a Claude-like tab 1.60 to 1.69 ms a second, about 41 µs a write of 391 B (about 108 ns a byte); the burst tab 16.34 ms a second, 25.4 µs a write of 248 B (105 ns a byte); the echo tab 0.72 ms a second, about 113 µs a key with its input. Its condrv messages (the I/O counters' "other") equal its writes. BENCH.md: 1.25 takes dense SGR 19 to 24% faster than 1.22, with about a quarter less of its own CPU. Run 5, real Claude Code's writes of 65 to 180 B, the clock at 1.07 of nominal: 61 to 64 µs a write (2.87 ms for 45 spinner writes, 6.43 for 105 while streaming, 5.09 for 81 in tool calls) | yes on Windows: a console program needs a console host, and its buffer has to be kept for programs that use the console API. LBTerm controls only which ConPTY is loaded (the bundled one, else the inbox one, silently: PITFALLS 106), the flags, the size (ResizePseudoConsole on every resize, with RESIZE_QUIRK), win32-input-mode for input, its answer to INHERIT_CURSOR's DSR at the start, and how fast it reads (backpressure); not how often or in what chunks it writes, which follows the program's calls | runs 1 and 5 (cycles, I/O counters, the recording), code, Microsoft's notes |
| 3 | The write lands in the anonymous output pipe's buffer in the kernel (NPFS), the bytes' first copy, completing the reader's pending ReadFile at once when it waits | `pty/src/win/conpty.rs`, `ConPtySystem::openpty` (filedescriptor's `Pipe::new`: CreatePipe with size 0, the system's default, synchronous) | kernel, in the conpty host's WriteFile | each ConPTY write | inside the conpty host's and the reader's; the pipe's real buffer quota not measured. With real Claude Code every write found the reader waiting: from its first prompt to the end of run 5, 248 ConPTY writes and 248 reads, the largest write 10,524 B | yes: ConPTY's only way out | code, run 5 |
| 4 | The reader's blocking ReadFile into its own 128 KB buffer, the second copy. A read takes whatever is queued, up to 128 KB, so writes queued while it was away come in one read | `mux/src/lib.rs`, `read_from_pane_pty` (`LBTERM_READ_BUFSIZE`, allocated zeroed once a pane); `filedescriptor/src/windows.rs`, `FileDescriptor::read` | server, the pane's reader thread | each read: 864 a second across run 1's 8 panes against 891 ConPTY writes, so 97% of the writes are read alone (at these rates reads hardly ever merge); real Claude Code's every write read alone, each of its frames one read (run 5) | with row 6's send, from the reader's cycles: 37 to 45 µs a read for a Claude-like tab (1.47 to 1.83 ms a second at 40 reads), 30.6 µs a read for the burst tab (19.67 ms a second at 643, twice its parser's 9.03). The readers were 98.5 to 100% blocked in NtReadFile. Estimated from the burst reader's samples (the send 1.5% of its time, about 23 µs a send): the ReadFile and its wake 5 to 10 µs, the send the rest | yes, something must read the pipe. Its own thread: doubtful. It exists because an anonymous pipe can't be waited on with a timeout, which the parser's coalescing wants; an overlapped named pipe (CreateNamedPipe with FILE_FLAG_OVERLAPPED in `openpty`) would let one thread read with a timeout and parse | run 1 (thread cycles, samples) |
| 5 | `histogram!("read_from_pane_pty.bytes.rate")` and a `log::trace!` of the read's size | `mux/src/lib.rs`, `read_from_pane_pty` | server, reader thread | each read | a few ns: the server installs no metrics recorder (`stats::Stats::init` is commented out in `wezterm-mux-server/src/main.rs`), so metrics' no-op recorder takes it, and the logger's level is Info by default (`env-bootstrap/src/ringlog.rs`), so the trace is an atomic load | no: nothing in the server can read the histogram (PITFALLS 99) | code |
| 6 | The read handed to the parser: `tx.write_all`, a winsock `send()` on filedescriptor's Windows socketpair, a loopback TCP connection (AF_INET on 127.0.0.1, bound, listened, connected and accepted in `socketpair_impl`; Nagle left on; SO_SNDBUF and SO_RCVBUF 1 MB). The third copy, into AFD and TCP; the delivery readies the parser if it's blocked in recv | `mux/src/lib.rs`, `read_from_pane_pty`, `allocate_socketpair`; `filedescriptor/src/windows.rs`, `socketpair_impl`, `FileDescriptor::write` | server, reader thread (the TCP loopback in its kernel time) | each read | most of row 4's 30 to 45 µs. A probe of Windows' loopback TCP with the same options (a .NET pair, 1 MB buffers, beside run 1, 15 trials a gap): a 300-byte send reached a blocked receiver in 55 to 99 µs at the median whatever the gap since the one before (0 to 150 ms), 152 µs at most, Nagle on or off: Nagle never held one. Up to about 2 MB can wait in the pair (1 MB each side) before send blocks, then the conpty host's WriteFile, then the program | no: the socket is only there so the parser can poll it with WSAPoll. An in-process hand-off (a queue of buffers with an event), or no hand-off at all with one overlapped reader (row 4), would do without the kernel round trip, two copies and the TCP stack | run 1, a loopback probe |
| 7 | The parser's `rx.read`, a winsock `recv()` into its own buffer of `mux_output_parser_buffer_size` (128 KB by default; Lachlan's config doesn't set it), the fourth copy. It blocks here whenever no output is coming | `mux/src/lib.rs`, `parse_buffered_data`; `filedescriptor/src/windows.rs`, `FileDescriptor::read` | server, the pane's parser thread | each time data is there: about one a ConPTY write at these rates (the burst tab's 10 writes a string are read as they come, inside one batch) | inside the parser's (row 13). Run 1's wall-clock samples: Claude-like parsers 93.7 to 94.7% blocked in recv, the burst parser 84.8% | no, as row 6: it's the socketpair's other end | run 1 |
| 8 | Parsed into actions: `Parser::parse` over vtparse's `VTParser::parse`. A run of two or more printable ASCII bytes in the ground state goes over whole (`VTActor::print_ascii` → `Action::PrintString(s.to_string())`, an allocation and the fifth copy, since `lbterm_print_runs` is on); any other byte goes through the state table one at a time (the UTF-8 decoder → `Action::Print(c)`); CSI as `Action::CSI` (`CSI::parse`), OSC as a boxed `OperatingSystemCommand` (vtparse keeps up to 1 KB of OSC in a heapless buffer), ESC, DCS, APC | `wezterm-escape-parser/src/parser/mod.rs`, `Parser::parse` and the Performer's `VTActor` impl (`print_ascii`); `vtparse/src/lib.rs`, `VTParser::parse`, `parse_byte` | server, parser thread | each recv, over its bytes | PERF.md, "What printing costs" (the model's test, `term/tests/lbterm_print.rs`, 2026-10-31): 3.0 ns a byte for plain lines, 13.3 for Claude Code's 24-bit look, 14.6 for the same in palette colours, 18.8 for dense SGR, 20.1 for wide characters and emoji; about 13 µs for a Claude-like frame of 960 B (estimated from those) | yes | PERF.md, code |
| 9 | In the parse's callback, each action: DECSET 2026 (synchronized output) starts a hold and flushes what came before it, the DECSET itself included, since the action is appended before the flush: so a frame that starts a read, as every one of Claude Code's does, is performed as two batches, the DECSET alone and then the frame at its DECRST (PITFALLS 125); DECRST 2026 or DECSTR (soft reset) ends it and flushes; then `Action::append_to(&mut actions)` merges consecutive prints into one PrintString (push_str or push, the sixth copy; a new `String::with_capacity(128)` when two Prints meet). A flush calls `send_actions_to_mux` at once (rows 11 to 16) and sets `action_size` to 0 | `mux/src/lib.rs`, `parse_buffered_data`'s callback; `wezterm-escape-parser/src/lib.rs`, `Action::append_to` | server, parser thread | each action; for Claude Code two batches a frame (run 5: 528 batches for 251 frames and 26 bursts outside them) | a match and a push each; the actions Vec grows again every batch, since `std::mem::take(&mut actions)` hands it, capacity and all, to `perform_actions`, which consumes it | the hold: yes, programs ask for it, but it has no time or size bound: held, actions grow past the 128 KB cap and nothing reaches the terminal until DECRST 2026 or a soft reset (PITFALLS 83, and 84 for the deadline it leaves behind). Claude Code (the installed build's binary) asks DECRQM 2026 and uses synchronized output when the status is 1, 2 or 3; LBTerm's terminal answers 2 (`term/src/terminalstate/mod.rs`, the `QueryDecPrivateMode(SynchronizedOutput)` arm), from under the terminal lock (row 13). Both pass through ConPTY 1.25 as written, and Claude Code 2.1.317 asks twice as it starts and then brackets every frame (run 5, row 1). The batch of the DECSET alone: no: it changes nothing (the terminal leaves the mode to the mux, its `SetDecPrivateMode(SynchronizedOutput)` arm empty) but takes the terminal lock, moves the seqno on and queues a PaneOutput task when none waits (rows 12 to 16); flushed before the DECSET is appended, what came before goes and the DECSET doesn't. The merge: yes, though its String copies could be borrowed slices (doubtful, small) | code, Claude Code's binary, run 5 |
| 10 | Coalescing: unless held, and while under 128 KB since the batch began, the first pass sets `deadline = now + mux_output_parser_coalesce_delay_ms` (3 ms by default, not set in Lachlan's config) after the parse. `Coalesce::wait` asks the socket with WSAPoll(0): readable, back to recv at once. If not, SetWaitableTimerEx on a `CREATE_WAITABLE_TIMER_HIGH_RESOLUTION` timer for the time left and WaitForSingleObject, sleeping the whole of it even if output comes sooner, then WSAPoll(0) again: readable, read and parse more, and the next pass finds the deadline gone and flushes. So every batch under 128 KB, an echo included, reaches the terminal 3 ms and the timer's slack after its first read | `mux/src/lib.rs`, `parse_buffered_data`; `mux/src/lbterm_wait.rs`, `Coalesce::wait`, `windows::Timer::sleep`; `filedescriptor/src/windows.rs`, `poll_impl` (WSAPoll) | server, parser thread (asleep in its timer) | each batch: 1 to 3 WSAPolls, one timer set, one wait | run 1's wall-clock samples: Claude-like parsers asleep in `Timer::sleep` 5.2 to 6.0% of the time at about 16 batches a second, 3.3 to 3.75 ms a batch (3 ms and 0.3 to 0.75 ms of slack); the burst parser 15.0% at about 65 a second, about 2.3 ms a batch (its deadline is set after the first read, so part of the window has gone). CPU estimated at 10 to 20 µs a batch for the polls, the timer and the wake. mux-load's echo over the same span: p50 9 ms, p95 16, max 79 over 320 keys, the 3 ms wait in every one. Real Claude Code's frames never wait on it: each is performed at its DECRST in the read it came in (run 5: 502 of 528 batches performed at a synchronized edge, after 0 µs); what waits the 3 ms is its output outside the brackets (its user var, title, OSC 7501 status and DECRQM queries, 26 batches in 40 s), and every program that brackets nothing, a shell's echo included | doubtful. Upstream added it to gather a frame that an unoptimized TUI writes in several writes, and ConPTY 1.22 and later still pass each console call through as its own pipe write µs apart. What decides it now: the server's pushes are already paced by what the client takes (`lbterm_flow`) and `OutputNotice` already folds notices; programs using DECSET 2026 skip it (Claude Code does, every frame: run 5); and it holds every echo 3 ms or more. A wait that ends when no more output has come for a short gap (an event-backed wait with the high-resolution timer, never WSAPoll's millisecond timeout, which ends on the 15.6 ms tick: PERF.md finding 67) would keep frames whole without holding a lone echo (PITFALLS 82) | runs 1 and 5, code |

| 37 | `Outbox::write_now`: a `send()` of everything not yet taken (uds_windows → Winsock's send → an AFD ioctl; afunix.sys copies it into the socket, whose SO_SNDBUF was set to 4 KB at accept). It sends until the socket has taken everything or says WouldBlock: a short write is followed by another `send()` at once, which usually meets WouldBlock, so a round makes one send when it all fits and two when the 4 KB buffer fills part way. What's left stays in the outbox, and `poll_writable` registers a noop waker (an AFD re-arm when the interest changes). Once all of it is taken the Vec is cleared (shrunk to 64 KB past 1 MB) and flushed (nothing for a unix stream). BrokenPipe, ConnectionReset or ConnectionAborted ends the connection quietly | `lbterm_flow.rs`, `Outbox::write_now`, `set_send_buffer`; `dispatch.rs`; async-io's `Async::poll_write`; uds_windows's `stdnet/socket.rs`; `local.rs` | server, main thread (the kernel does the copy) | once a round: one send, or two when the socket's buffer fills part way | 2 to 5 µs a send, one ioctl and a copy of at most about 6 KB (estimated); not separable in the samples, and the process's I/O counters can't isolate it (run 2's 384.8 other I/O operations a second include the parsers' socketpairs, row 6). ### 3. The GUI, from the socket to the window invalidated

| # | Step | Where | Process, thread | Runs | Costs | Needs to run | Found out by |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 42 | The client thread's loop waits on a GUI request or the socket, `smol::future::or(rx.recv(), wait_for_read)`, after checking whether its reading is held (row 52) | `wezterm-client/src/client.rs`, `client_thread_async` | GUI, the mux client thread | each turn: once a PDU decoded and once a request sent | well under 1 µs a turn (estimated); `rx.recv()` makes an async-channel Recv each turn and, with the channel empty, allocates an event-listener node, dropped when the read side wins; the wait for the socket boxes its future too (row 43), and the decode and the encode go through `dyn` AsyncRead and AsyncWrite | yes: one place has to wait on both. A new Recv and listener each turn: doubtful, a receiver kept across turns would do | code |
| 43 | The wait for readable: `stream.wait_for_readable()`, the `AsyncReadAndWrite` trait's `#[async_trait(?Send)]` method called on the `Box<dyn AsyncReadAndWrite>` the client keeps, so every wait boxes its future (a `Pin<Box<dyn Future>>` round `Async<UnixStream>::readable()`, one allocation a wait); then through async-io 2.6 and polling 3.11's IOCP backend: the socket's AFD poll re-armed (IOCTL_AFD_POLL, one NtDeviceIoControlFile) and, when this thread holds async-io's reactor, a wait in GetQueuedCompletionStatusEx there (`block_on`'s `react(None)`). A GUI request, or any async-io timer the GUI arms, interrupts it through `Reactor::notify` (PostQueuedCompletionStatus). When `react` returns with nothing for this thread after it has held the reactor over 500 µs (every timer's notify after a wait), it lets the reactor go, unparks the `async-io` thread and parks. When the `async-io` thread holds the reactor instead (it takes it by itself after about 20 ms with no tick: "The processes and threads it crosses"), this thread's `try_lock` fails and it parks, and a socket event reaches it as GetQueuedCompletionStatusEx on the `async-io` thread and an unpark | `client.rs`, `AsyncReadAndWrite::wait_for_readable` for `Async<T>`; async-io's `driver.rs` (`block_on`, `main_loop`) and `reactor.rs`; polling's `iocp/mod.rs` (`update_packet`, `wait_deadline`) | GUI, client thread, or the `async-io` thread for it; whichever holds async-io's reactor also fires the GUI thread's async-io timers (PITFALLS 107) | only when the PduReader has nothing buffered: once a socketful, not once a PDU | an allocation, 2 to 3 syscalls and a thread wake, about 5 to 15 µs with the context switch (estimated), and a second thread's wake when the `async-io` thread holds the reactor; not split out by the counters, and which thread holds the reactor isn't known ("Not traced yet") | a wait: yes. What a line's quads take from its row, and where each goes since perf/line-quads-by-content (rows 85 to 87; nothing may be drawn at another row's place, PITFALLS 128):

- **Its pixel y**: the pane's top (`top_pixel_y` in `paint_pane`: the tab bar, the padding, the border), the pane's top row in its tab (`pos.top`) and the line's index on the screen, times the cell height. Added as it's copied (`row_y`, row 86). Every quad `render_screen_line` makes is placed from `params.top_pixel_y`, which is 0 for the layout: the backgrounds and their clip to the line (`bounding_rect`), reverse video's fill, underlines, the selection's and the composition's backgrounds, the cursor (`pos_y`, a password's lock glyph too), every glyph (`pos_y + top`), a double-height line's two halves (drawn by its top line, `height_scale`), and images (`populate_image_quad`'s own `pos_y`). So each moves by `row_y` and nothing else.
- **The window's size**, in every y (`pos_y` is `-pixel_height / 2 + top_pixel_y`, `filled_rectangle`'s `top_offset`) and every x (`gl_x`): in the layout, and so in the key through `quad_generation`, which a resize moves on (`termwindow/resize.rs`).
- **The pane's left edge** (`left_pixel_x`): in the key, as on main. A pane moved sideways (a split) is laid out again.
- **The cursor's row** (`cursor.y == stable_row`): in the key, `cursor` (its x, shape, visibility and colours, a dead key or leader, its y fixed at 0), with the composing text and `password_input`, both the cursor's row's alone. Inside the layout `stable_line_idx` is read only to find the cursor's row (`cursor_idx`, `cursor_cell`, `cursor_range`) and to tell the retro tab bar (`None`), both of which the key carries.
- **The selection's columns on the row** (`SelectionRange::cols_for_row(stable_row)`, clipped to the pane's width): in the key, `selection`.
- **The keyboard**: in the key, `pane_is_active` and `lbterm_focused` (`lbterm_extras::work::line_keyboard`: with the compositor, only the cursor's line goes by it).
- **Triggers**: `lbterm_trigger_line` is keyed by (pane, stable row) and the line's seqno, and returns a recoloured copy, whose shape hash is the key's; the rainbow's columns (`lbterm_rainbow_gradient`) are that line's matches, set for it by the same call. Both come from what the line says and the rules (the config generation), not where it is.
- **Links under the pointer**: in the value (its links and the highlight it was drawn with, `lbterm_extras::link_looks::hover_changed`), as on main; not the row.
- **A picture row's thumbnail**: drawn apart. The row's runs are noted before the lookup, by its index (`lbterm_thumbnail_runs`), and drawn after every row at their rows (`lbterm_paint_thumbnails`).
- **Colour codes** (`#RRGGBB`, lbterm/HEX.md, since feat/hex-swatch): nothing from the row. A miss's layout (row 87, `render_screen_line`) walks the row's own cells once into `lbterm_extras::hex::Finder` (`render/hex.rs`, `lbterm_hex_bars`; each cell's text against `#` or a hex digit and its underline, hidden and wrapped bits, about a hundred cells, worked out at under a microsecond of a layout that costs tens, not measured alone) and adds a bar in each code's colour, and its outline when the colour is too near the background, to the row's quads, in layer 0 after the selection's background. The walk is the layout's only extra look at the cells: `Line::last_cell_was_wrapped`, which walks a screen row again, isn't asked (PITFALLS 290). A code is a function of the cells, which the key holds through `shape_hash`, the switch (`lbterm_hex_colours`) is in its config generation, the palette in its shape generation and the DPI and columns in its quad generation (HEX.md, "A code too near its background", has the palette change's path), so the quads kept are right whenever the key matches: a still screen and a row that only moved look at nothing. It runs again on every miss of a row whose text didn't change (the cursor's rows on each key, a selection's, a hovered link's, focus, blinking text), each inside a full layout. Against main's build (2026-11-09, PERF.md, "Colour codes, by `budgets -Against`"): `repaint` laid out no row on either build, and `busy-scroll` (about 5 rows laid out a second) and `throughput` (about 200 a run) were the same on the GUI thread and every other metric but `throughput`'s refresh count, one higher by a count's floor while its time was the same. Not at the link scan (row 79's `scan_rows`, `KeptScans`): the scan's crates can't reach `lbterm-extras`, and what it found would have to be put on the line or its cells and cleared at every change to them (HEX.md, "Why not where the links are found").
- **Blinking text and animated images**: the value's `expires`, as on main.
- **`phys_line_idx`**: nothing in the layout read it; it was in the key only. Gone.
- **The line's width in cells** (`pixel_width`: a background's clip, a glyph's overflow, the retro tab bar's last cell): not in the key, as on main; a line whose cells fit draws the same at any width it fits.

### A title out of view (◐ to ◑), from its three PDUs to the GUI thread's last step

A working Claude Code session (2.1.312) sets its title every 960 ms, `◐` and `◑` in turn before its words, so with Lachlan's 8 working tabs about 8 titles a second reach the window, nearly all of them of tabs out of view, and none changes anything on the screen: the rail strips the glyph from a row's words and reads both as working (`lbterm_panel::claude::titles_move_row`). Traced on 2026-11-10 (perf/background-titles' stage 1) on main's 3eb92cc95, from the code and run 16 below; rows t1 to t3 are on the GUI's client thread, t4 to t14 on its GUI thread, which every key and frame waits on. MUX.md's "What a title costs" has the history of this path; What could go 13 is its redesign.

What the server sends for one (rows 20, 21 and 26; MUX.md, "What goes at once, without lines"): a pane out of view on a connection whose window said SetShownPanes owes `Owed::names` for its title alert, so its title goes as a names push, a GetPaneRenderChangesResponse with no lines, no rows named and the cursor, size, flags and seqno as last sent, 66 bytes (`lbterm_out_of_view`, sent only when the title or folder differs from what the connection last sent); then the NotifyAlert, `Alert::WindowTitleChanged(title)`, 13 bytes; and, since the server's mux window takes the title of whichever pane set one last, a WindowTitleChanged for the mux window, 12 bytes, which `Pending` holds once a window (its latest). In run 16, 83 titles brought 75 names pushes, 75 NotifyAlerts and 82 WindowTitleChanged (84, 84 and 84 for 84 titles in the second phase): in the first phase each of the 8 tabs began with the title it had (`◐ <name>`), set again, which raises no alert and sends no push but turns the server's window title (MUX.md), so 8 fewer pushes and alerts, and two titles within one round of the connection left the window's title once.

| # | Step | Where | Process, thread | Runs | Costs | Needs to run | Found out by |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| t1 | Read and decode each of the three PDUs, counted and logged (rows 42 to 48): a names push decodes no lines, so `hydrate_lines` later has nothing to do | `client.rs`, `client_thread_async`; codec | GUI, client thread | three PDUs a title | a few µs each (estimated) | yes: they're the news | code, run 16 |
| t2 | Each handed to the GUI thread (row 49): `process_unilateral` checks the type (WindowTitleChanged is one of the window-level PDUs it lets through), then `backlog.hand`: the unapplied count, the State mutex, `Merger::add`. A names push of a pane whose earlier push still waits untaken merges with it (row 50), the newer's title winning | `client.rs`, `process_unilateral`; `lbterm_backlog.rs`, `Backlog::hand` | GUI, client thread | three a title | under 1 µs each (estimated) | handing the names push: no, and since perf/background-titles' stage 2 it isn't: `lbterm_names::applied_here` applies it on this thread when nothing of its pane waits in the backlog and it changes nothing but the title, folder and seqno (t6), and it goes no further. The alert: only when the GUI would do something for it; since perf/background-titles' stage 3 the window's mux subscription drops one that wouldn't (t8), but it was still handed and taken to reach the subscription (What could go 13, "Part 2 as built"); since perf/background-titles' fix-up it's decided on the client thread before `backlog.hand` and dropped there with nothing handed, when nothing of its pane waits (`lbterm_titles::decided_here`; "Parts 2 and 3 on the client thread, as built"). The window's title: since the fix-up kept on the client thread (mux's `lbterm_window_titles`) and handed only while something reads one as it changes (part 3) | code |

Everything else: no, for a push that carries nothing but them. On the GUI thread: no. Built in perf/background-titles' stage 2: on the client thread, `applied_here` does row 57's lookups (no resync: a pane the window doesn't know yet goes the GUI way, which resyncs), then `ClientPane::lbterm_names_here` compares the two flags and, under the renderable's lock, `RenderableInner::lbterm_names_here`: `lbterm_names::current` (the cursor and size as last applied, a seqno not behind the last applied, no hold it would end, PITFALLS 311) and no local echo guessing (`should_predict`), then sets what the apply would (`poll.pushed`, `last_recv_time`, the title, the folder, the seqno, `Drawn::applied`; said at once, as the apply says one, when it changes nothing) with no PaneOutput. Its cost moves to the client thread: five locks and three Arc clones for the lookups, the backlog's mutex, the pane's three locks, a String compare and clone: a few µs (estimated), and a wait for the renderable's lock while the GUI thread holds it (a Lua read, hidden_scan, the rail's `get_title`). Anything else goes the GUI way, as before | code |

The budget's news: only for work that runs, so a title alert the subscription drops (t8) brings none, and needs none: no GUI work runs for it to excuse | run 16, code |
| t14 | `lbterm_title_alert` for a pane out of view: `taskbar::retry_due` (a map lookup); `lbterm_holds_pane` (`get_window`, `iter_tabs`, each tab's split tree walked by `contains_pane`); for a title, `lbterm_panel_title_changed`: `tab_panel_drawn` (the walk again, then `lbterm_panel.drawn`'s get), `get_active_pane`, `iter_panes_ignoring_zoom`, `pane_titles` (each pane's `get_title`: its renderable lock and a String clone), `tab_panel_now` (SystemTime), `titles_move_row`: the pane titles differ (◐ against ◑), so the drawn `Tab` cloned and `row` worked out twice (`strip_glyphs`, `program_name`, `claude_glyph`, the row's words and state), and twice more without the link when the row has one: false, nothing painted; a debug line's level check. Then `lbterm_pane_changed`: `get_active_tab_for_window` and `contains_pane`, `tab_bar_shows_tabs`, `has_handler("format-window-title")` and `status_handlers()` (two more `has_handler`), each `run_immediate_with_lua_config` (the thread-local Lua context's `update_to_latest`) and `has_event_handler` (a read of wezterm.on's registry through mlua); `titles::wants_update` false: nothing scheduled | `termwindow/titles.rs`, `lbterm_title_alert`, `lbterm_pane_changed`, `has_handler`; `render/tab_panel.rs`, `lbterm_panel_title_changed`, `tab_panel_drawn`; `lbterm-panel/src/claude.rs`, `titles_move_row`, `row_since`; `taskbar.rs`, `retry_due`; `config/src/lib.rs`, `run_immediate_with_lua_config` | GUI, GUI thread | once a title alert of a pane of this window | inside t13's 45 µs (not split; the row clones and the three Lua lookups most of it, estimated) | the decision: yes, it's what keeps a title that changes a row, the caption or a handler's input working. Made on the GUI thread: no: its inputs are the mux's maps and the pane's title (Sync), and two the GUI thread alone has, the rail's rows as last drawn and the handlers' answers, which it can leave where the client thread reads them (What could go 13, part 2). The three `has_handler` lookups every title: no, the answers change only at a config load, when `lbterm_titles_read` is already asked. Since perf/background-titles' stage 3: the decision's inputs are left where the subscription reads them (`TitleWatch`, t8), which drops a title alert of a background tab before any task when nothing here would change, so ◐ and ◑ don't get here; for one that does, `lbterm_pane_changed` and `lbterm_title_alert` take the handlers' answers from the watch, asked at each config load or override (`lbterm_titles_read_update`, three to five lookups a load), and `titles_move_row` answers ◐ against ◑ without making a row (t8) | code |

All of it, in run 16's titles phases: 371 and 389 tasks (4.5 and 4.6 a title) for 9.47 and 10.25 ms, 166 and 172 wakes, no frame and no timer, and 12.9 and 13.6 ms of the GUI thread's cycles, 0.155 and 0.162 ms a title (the rest phase before them: 2 tasks, 0.2 ms). The GUI process spent 59.3 and 57.5 ms in the 10 s against 23.4 at rest, and the server 31.3 and 30.9 ms (0.37 ms a title, its parse included). The 0.155 ms is what perf/title-storm.ps1's 4% budget holds against a frame of the open tab, now 3 to 4 ms: none of it draws anything.

### Where it costs most

- Out of view. Under Lachlan's kind of load (run 3: six Claude-like tabs and two bursting tabs out of view, an echo in view), the GUI thread spent about 21% of a core applying pushes (row 54), 97% of them for tabs out of view, most of it scanning each line for links as it comes (row 60, by perf/mux-client's timing build), and the client thread 14.5% decoding them (rows 42 to 52, the split unknown). Each notice of a pane out of view costs the window two tasks for nothing (rows 61, 64 and 65).
- The server. Its main thread spent 39 to 60 ms a second (runs 1 and 2), and in run 2 0.38 to 0.40 ms a push with keys, polls and notices included: nearly half the encode (row 35), a fifth the cells rebuilt (row 32), the rest the lines' copies, the proc list for panes that say no cwd, and the machinery. Each Claude-like pane's reader and parser took 3.7 to 4.2 ms a second between them, the larger part the hand-offs, polls and wakes rather than the parse (rows 4 to 13), and its conpty host 1.6 to 1.7 ms.
- The server under a flood (run 6). Its pushes follow the output, not the window: a pane is pushed once a parser batch, whichever window reads it, and panes out of view took 99.8% of the flood's push time (472.7 of 473.7 ms a second) and 99% of mux-load's (113.7 of 114.9). A flooding pane's push took 464 µs: 30% its cells rebuilt (row 32), 40% the encode (row 35), 24% its reads (row 27: its cwd read again at every push, and the terminal lock waited for behind the parser), 4% the lines' copy. Only with a flooding tab in view does the window's pace show: the installed window fell behind its socket, so each push covered more, where this window took about 1.6 times the pushes for about the same lines (What could go 12).
- A scrolling frame. A frame of a mux pane that scrolls took 9.3 to 9.9 ms of the GUI thread, 8.85 of it the paint and 6.9 the rows (95% of the pass): every row laid out again because the line quad cache's key holds the row's position (rows 85, 87 and 91), a quarter of the paint in the heap for the Box a quad and the copies (row 93), every row hashed again (row 84), the whole layer, 3.8 MB, uploaded (row 98), and a poll a frame (row 76). That frame was a stand-in's, a line printed every frame; real Claude Code draws one like it only when its conversation grows, about once a tool call, and its other frames, 5.5 to 14 a second, rewrite a row in place and pay the copy of every row's quads (row 86) and their hashes (row 84) all the same (run 5). On main; perf/line-quads-by-content keys the line quad cache by what a line shows and copies a kept line in as one block moved to its row, so a scroll lays out only the rows it brings and no quad is built or boxed again (rows 85 to 87, 91 and 93, What could go 3), the hashing of row 84 and the upload of row 98 as they were: in `busy-scroll`, a smaller pane than run 4's, the pass 2.42 ms on main against 1.13 and the GUI thread 41.2 ms a second against 31.3 (PERF.md finding 97).
- Every echo waits out the parser's 3 ms coalescing and its slack (row 10).

### Where the traces disagree, or leave a seam

- **The lines' form on the wire.** Segment 2's trace found the server's `SerializedLines::from` turning every line back into cells (row 32), so pushes carry `CellStorage::V`. Segment 3's trace took the lines to arrive clustered, and so had the decode build ClusteredLines with an Arc a cluster (row 46), the backlog's merge turn the merged lines into cells, and merged pushes' lines held several times larger in the client's cache than unmerged ones, perhaps MUX.md's 2.9 KB a cached line. The code sides with segment 2 (read by the writer): `From<Vec<(StableRowIndex, Line)>>` calls `cells_mut_for_attr_changes_only` on every line, which is `coerce_vec_storage`, nothing compresses them again before `serialize_with`, and the line and cell types' serde is derived, so the client decodes cells. Every line in a client pane's cache is then in cell storage, merged or not, which would fit 2.9 KB a line of 120 columns at about 24 B a cell; and `scan_and_create_hyperlinks` builds each line's text as a String, since `as_str` borrows only a clustered line's. Rows 46, 50 and 60 are written to the code, and segment 3's pitfall that merged lines are bigger isn't written as one (PITFALLS 91 keeps what stands of it). Neither form was measured.
- **Terminal locks a push.** Segment 1 counted about nine a push and segment 2 seven (nine with the cursor's row and a program status). The code: nine for a push that sends (the seven getters of row 27, `get_changed_since` and `lines_to_send`), eight when nothing changed, one more for a cursor row outside the viewport, and one each for a program status and a palette (row 34).
- **Push rates, and the GUI thread's cost a push.** The three mux-load runs saw 103 pushes a second (run 2, counted from codec=debug) and 240 (run 3, from the window's metrics; MUX.md's run on perf/mux-server 238). Run 3's GUI thread spent 0.89 ms a push and 210 ms a second on main, where perf/mux-client's MUX.md measured main (6cc0bc7ef, with perf/key-latency) under the same scenario at 3.2 to 6.8 ms a push of tabs out of view and 590 to 597 ms a second. Neither difference was explained: the stand-ins shared the warden job's 4 CPUs at below-normal priority in these runs, and main has changed since 6cc0bc7ef.
- **Which thread is the client's.** Run 3 found the client thread unnamed, by elimination (1,453 ms in 10 s), and the `async-io` thread under 15.6 ms. Run 4's thread list has `async-io` at 47 ms and "the client reactor" at 31 ms in 11.3 s, with two unnamed threads at 15.6 each, and doesn't say how it named the reactor; segment 4's trace had the poll's PDU carried by "the client's async-io thread". The rows follow segment 3's in that the client thread writes the GUI's requests itself. Segment 3's trace also had the `async-io` thread take the reactor only on the client thread's hand-over; async-io 2.6's code says otherwise (it takes it by itself after about 20 ms with no tick: "The processes and threads it crosses"), and which thread holds it isn't known, which also fits run 4's `async-io` at 47 ms against run 3's under 15.6. Which of run 4's threads was the client thread isn't joined.
- **The open pane's notice.** In run 4, where every notice was the open pane's, the window's second hop (row 65) ran 329 times for 12.0 ms, 36 µs a run, against 3.65 µs in run 3, where 97% were out of view; the subscription's hop 4.6 µs a run against 2.5. In the code the open pane's notice differs from a hidden pane's only by the invalidate (row 67): `lbterm_budget_notif` does as much or less for it and `is_pane_visible` runs for both. So the gap is InvalidateRect, about 30 µs, unless a timing shows otherwise (row 67; "Not traced yet").
- **The poll's push.** Segment 2 couldn't say how many of run 2's pushes answered polls rather than being owed. Run 4's counts (329 pushes, 328 polls, 330 frames) suggest the polls brought no push of their own there, the owed push having sent the change already; that's one scenario's counts, not a count of the poll's pushes.
- **Alerts.** No run raised one (no stand-in set a title or user var), so the alert rows (14, 20, 21 and 34) are from the code, and the GUI's side of an alert (NotifyAlert in row 58, `Mux::notify(Alert)`, the window's Alert arms in `dispatch_notif`, the rail's repaint) is joined only in CODEMAP.md's outline ("Pane output", step 3), not traced as rows.

### On the live branches

- perf/mux-client (built, main merged in on 2026-11-05; its rows above say what changed, each with a 'Since perf/mux-client') changes the GUI's half: `put_line` doesn't scan (row 60), `ClientPane::apply_hyperlinks` and `scan_rows` scan held rows in place as they're drawn, and a quiet hidden tab's screen 250 ms after the last push that brought it rows (`hidden_scan`; rows 60, 61 and 79); the window's subscriber takes a mux pane's output outside the open tab as the budget's news with no task (`lbterm_hidden_output`; rows 61, 64 and 65); a push or input no longer brings the poll back on a unix domain (`lbterm_poll.rs`, PollPace; rows 59 and 76); the frame is lent the rows where they're held, their shape key kept and only changed rows copied (`lbterm_lent.rs`; rows 79, 81 and 84); `read_row` takes a line newer than its push at the first fetch (row 63); `all_stale` changes entries where they are (row 58: SetPalette's `make_all_stale`, which a resize and a zoom also run); a write to a pane (WriteToPane, row 39) is sent from a task and not waited for; moves of the pointer over a program without the mouse aren't sent, and the server answers a mouse event with no push (row 39). It leaves the line quad cache's key, the Box a quad (rows 85 to 91), the client thread's decode and the server's side as they are on main. Its server times each push it owes, step by step, and a server a test window started logs them once a second (`lbterm_pushstats.rs`, `lbterm::push`), which run 6 read. Its flood build (stage 3, 2026-11-07) changes the server's half for the first time: rows 27 to 29 read the pane under one lock with the cwd as listed, and an owe of alerts alone that don't read the push is sent without the pane read (What could go 12, its 'Beside it').
- perf/gpu-memory (landed on main, 2026-11-04) changes rows 89, 90, 97 and 98 only. On D3D12 the GPU allocator's blocks are smaller than wgpu's 256 MB device and 64 MB upload blocks (`MemoryHints::Manual` with `config::lbterm::GPU_MEMORY_BLOCK`, 128 MB, its upload blocks half that; other backends keep wgpu's default), and a glyph's and a layer's staging buffers come from them: each new glyph the atlas takes is staged in an upload buffer of its own, 64 KB at least, so a burst of new glyphs makes and lets go blocks, and smaller blocks make a refill of the atlas slower (PITFALLS 77; PERF.md, "What a window's GPU memory holds"). D3D12's view heap holds 65,536 views (`GPU_VIEW_DESCRIPTORS`), where wgpu's default limit made it 1,000,000, 30.5 MB of the GPU's memory mapped into the process. A uniform is a 64 KB D3D12 buffer whatever its size (PITFALLS 111), built for the window's size, so a frame drawn at a third size lets go of the uniforms from the size two back (`lbterm_motion::content::UniformsAt`; PITFALLS 112), and those kept are capped by memory at `GPU_UNIFORMS_KEPT`. Nothing in rows 1 to 88.
- perf/line-quads-by-content (built and reviewed, its fix-up of 2026-11-04 with main merged in; not landed) changes rows 83 to 87, 91 and 93, written here as they become: the line quad cache keyed by what a line shows, a line laid out at row 0 and kept as flat vertices with their hashes, moved to its row as it's copied in (What could go, 3). Its first stage measured real Claude Code's output (run 5, rows 1 to 4, 9, 10 and 13).

### What could go

The flow as it should be, designed on 2026-11-04 from the rows whose need is no or doubtful and from what the flow as a whole does more than once (lines compressed and turned back into cells, links taken off and put back twice, a frame's rows copied three times, a line fetched twice), holds too long (a pane's terminal lock through a process-list ask, ON_MAIN_THREAD through a wake, the client pane's lock through `Mux::notify`) or hands between threads for nothing (the reader to the parser over loopback TCP, the parser to the server's main thread and on to the connection, a notice's two tasks, the GUI's timers fired on the client thread). Work removed, joined or moved, never a value changed. Held to Lachlan's daily load: 8 to 18 Claude Code tabs on the mux domain, most out of view, the open one printing; the GUI thread's time above all, since it takes his typing too. Largest saving first. Nothing here is built: Lachlan decides first. The savings are worked out from the rows' costs, which came from pwsh stand-ins, not real Claude Code (whose write and redraw pattern is in "Not traced yet"), and his 18 tabs would be about twice mux-load's eight.

1. **perf/mux-client, designed and measured there, not designed again here** (built; the rows say what changed). A line is scanned for links as it's drawn, not as it comes (`ClientPane::apply_hyperlinks`, `scan_rows`, and a quiet hidden tab's screen 250 ms after its last output, `hidden_scan`); a mux pane's output out of the open tab is the budget's news in the window's subscriber, with no task; a push or input no longer brings the poll back on a unix domain (`lbterm_poll.rs`); the frame is lent the rows where they're held, their shape key kept and only changed rows copied (`lbterm_lent.rs`); a line newer than its push is fetched once (`read_row`); `all_stale` changes entries in place; a write to a pane goes from a task; pointer moves over a program without the mouse aren't sent.
   - Rows: 39's poll push, 58's `make_all_stale`, 59, 60, 61 and 64 to 65 for panes out of view, 63, 76, 79, 81 and 84.
   - Saves (its MUX.md, measured against main's 6cc0bc7ef): under mux-load the window's GUI thread 54 and 58 ms a second against 590 and 597, its process 386 and 414 against 968 and 956, a key's echo 12 and 13 ms at the median against 20 and 19; a push of a tab out of view 132 to 178 µs of the GUI thread against 3,163 to 6,771; a mux pane's frame 4.2 to 4.6 ms against 6.0 to 6.3; 1 to 6 polls in 6 s against 51.
   - Could break, and what Lachlan would notice: one evening's mux-flood runs lost a key in 6 of 17, never shown to be its and not since; a switch to a tab still working scans its screen in its first frame (about 3 ms for 36 lines of URLs); with a flooding tab in view the server works out about 1.6 times the pushes for about the same lines, because this window reads each as it comes where the installed one fell behind its socket (run 6: 1,135 against 712 a second, 511 against 335 ms a second of its main thread); with the floods out of view, and under mux-load, it pushes the same either way, once a parser batch a pane, and the 'ten times the pushes' of 2026-11-01 was the window's own task count (MUX.md; What could go 12). He'd notice a lighter window with many sessions working.
   - Size: 26 files, 2,246 lines in; upstream's `renderable.rs`, `clientpane.rs`, `termwindow/mod.rs`, `pane_lines.rs`, `copy.rs` and `quickselect.rs`, the rest LBTerm's own files. Parked; main has moved since.
   - It leaves the rest of this list as it is: the line quad cache's key, the Box a quad, the client thread's decode, the server's side, and a push of every pane out of view crossing three threads.
   - Where it ended, against the installed build (Run 8, 2026-11-08, after its flood build, close-pane stage, failed writes and its scan of a hidden tab's screen no longer taking a title for news): the server's main thread under the lines flood 382 ms a second against 316 (1.2 times, where it was about 2 on 2026-11-01), with a flood in view 335 against 301, under the alerts flood 72 against 157, under mux-load 133 against 128; the window's GUI thread 227 against 624 under the lines flood and 43 against 346 under mux-load, a key's echo beside the flood 28 ms at the median against 47. What's left of the server's extra under the lines flood is the hidden panes' pushes this window reads as they come (proposal 2 takes them away) and the cells and encode of each (rows 32 and 35). Run 9 (the review's, two runs each in turn) found Run 8's 1.2 times inside the runs' spread with the floods out of view (about 1.05) and 1.2 to 1.45 times with a flood in view. Run 10 (the fix-up's, the floods counting what they wrote) settles it per line: the pair does 1.2 to 1.25 times the installed pair's main thread a line out of view and 1.35 to 1.4 in view, for the same lines written, and crossing the builds puts all of it on the window's pace (this window makes either server push more, smaller pushes), none on the server's code, which does about 0.8 times the installed server's work a line with the same window. Proposal 2 removes it for panes out of view and What could go 12's main design for the one shown; both need the PDU proposal 2 adds, so neither is built on this branch.
2. **Out of view, a pane's output stops at the server.** The server pushes a pane's lines to a window only while the window shows it: the panes of each of its mux windows' active tab, which SetFocusedPane already tells the server, said at the switch rather than at the new tab's first frame (`advise_focus` is in the paint, row 75). A pane out of view stays owed in `Pending`, as it is today under backpressure. Its alerts (user vars, program status, bells, unseen output, which the rail and Lua's events read) and its title go at once, without lines. Its lines go in one push carrying everything since: when it's shown, when it has been quiet for a while (a session done and waiting then has its screen current before he switches to it), or when the window asks.
   - Rows, for a pane out of view: 25 to 38 (the push worked out, encoded and sent), 41, 42 to 52 (read, decoded, merged and handed over), 53 to 66 (taken, applied, noticed). With them go row 54's GAP and row 68's frame waiting behind a drain of pushes, the window's budget news for that output (rows 61 and 65), and perf/mux-client's `hidden_scan` (a pane is scanned when its push comes).
   - Saves, at mux-load's 240 pushes a second, 97% of them out of view (run 3): the server's main thread about 85 ms a second (0.36 to 0.40 ms a push: run 2, and MUX.md's 86 to 88 ms a second for 238); the client thread about 140 of its 145 ms a second (573 µs a PDU, run 3); the GUI thread about 200 ms a second on main (0.89 ms a push), or about 36 after perf/mux-client (132 to 178 µs a push), and about 170 of its 178 wakes a second. About a quarter of a core in all after perf/mux-client, all day, at half his tab count. Measured by run 6 (the server timing each push, 2026-11-07): panes out of view took 472.7 of the server's 473.7 ms a second of pushes under mux-flood's flood, 452.6 of 521.2 with a flooding tab in view, and 113.7 of 114.9 under mux-load, so this removes nearly all the server's push work at both loads. What it leaves: the shown pane's pushes (What could go 12), and the alerts and titles of panes out of view, which still go at once: about 10 ms a second of the flood's pushes were those PDUs' encodes (`rest`), and under `-Kind alerts`, where 85% of a push was waiting for the pane's terminal lock to read it (row 27), a push of alerts without lines reads nothing.
   - Could break, and what Lachlan would notice: a switch to a tab still printing finds its screen as last pushed until the shown push lands, a round trip of 0.3 to 5 ms here inside a switch's 31 to 41 ms to its first frame, so the first frame either waits for it or draws the old screen for a frame (his call). Anything in the GUI that reads a hidden pane's lines reads them as last pushed. On main that's nothing in his config (no Lua reads lines, the Conductor's view draws the seat, not panes), a test's `cli get-text` through the GUI's own socket, and `hidden_scan`. A newer server has to go on pushing every pane to a GUI that doesn't say what it shows (`rollback`), so the GUI says it with a new PDU, which an older server must not end the connection on. He'd notice much less CPU and heat with many sessions working, and possibly a frame of a tab's old screen as he switches to one mid-stream.
   - The other place it could stop: the client thread keeps a hidden pane's merged push (`lbterm_backlog` merges them already) and hands it to the GUI thread only when the pane is shown, read (a ClientPane read takes it first) or its title, size or flags change. That saves the GUI thread's part alone, with no round trip, and needs the merge to move lines as Lines (row 50, PITFALLS 91), since all of a hidden pane's pushes would then merge. perf/mux-client kept the server's pushes because holding them there "would show its old screen for a round trip on the switch" (its MUX.md); this is the design that answers it.
   - Size: the server's `lbterm_flow.rs` (a shown set and a quiet deadline in `Pending`), `dispatch.rs` (the quiet timer), `sessionhandler.rs` and codec (the new PDU); the client's `clientpane.rs` and `termwindow` (say what's shown at the switch). Mostly LBTerm's own files, one wire change.
   - Decided (Lachlan, 2026-11-04, his 10a and 11a): it stops at the server, not on the client thread, and a switch to a tab still printing has its first frame wait for that pane's push, up to a bound, rather than show the screen as last pushed for a frame. Built after perf/mux-client lands, which it builds on.
   - Built (perf/hidden-output, 2026-11-08, in five stages; not landed): designed in MUX.md, "Tabs out of view: output held at the server" (what the window says and when, what the server holds, what goes at once, when held lines go, the switch's bound of 50 ms, each pairing with an older build, and room in the PDU for 12). Two PDUs, not one: `SetShownPanes` (7505, window to server, the whole set shown, each pane with a `drawn` seqno for 12) and `PaneHeld` (7506, server to window, once a hold, only to a window that has said the first: PITFALLS 188), so a switch waits only for a pane that is behind, with no round trip asked at every switch. Stage 1 built both in codec and the server's answer, which notes the set in the connection's `Pending` (row 39) and changes no push yet. Stage 2 built the server's holds (MUX.md, "Built so far"): on a connection whose window says what it shows, rows 25 to 38 run for a pane out of view only for the word of its hold, its alerts, its title or folder alone, and the push that ends the hold, everything since in one; rows 21, 25, 26, 29, 38 and 39 say how. Stage 3 built the window's side (MUX.md, "The window's side, as built"; its tests ran and passed in stage 5, the warden having refused them for disk until then): the client domain's subscriber (row 61's fourth kind, which did nothing for these notifications) marks on WindowInvalidated, TabResized, TabAddedToWindow, WindowCreated, WindowRemoved, PaneRemoved and ActiveWorkspaceChanged and queues one task a burst, which sends SetShownPanes when the set changed; PaneHeld is applied as a pane's PDU (rows 53 to 56), judged against the seqno applied (PITFALLS 190), and a push past it ends the hold before its PaneOutput (row 61); every paint asks first whether a new view's first frame waits (row 71's paint: the active tab's lookup a frame, the panes' holds asked only at a new view), and the window crate held such a frame on its high-resolution timer without a pacing period (until the second round's stage 2, which holds no paint and draws the tab drawn last's panes in the new view's place instead, Lachlan's 2b); hidden_scan scans at once a push that ended a hold, with no timer (row 61's `hidden_scan::output`). Under mux-flood's flood out of view rows 42 to 66 then run for no busy tab's lines at all: its PaneHeld once, its alerts and titles. Stage 4 gave perf/mux-hidden.ps1, mux-load.ps1 and tab-switch.ps1 their pairings with the installed build, and stage 5 ran them and fixed what they got wrong (PITFALLS 191 and 195; perf-scripts.md): mux-hidden passes in this build's pair and in both pairings with the installed build, the window's connection never ending. Measured in run 11, each build twice in turn: under mux-load the server's main thread 6.6 and 7.8 ms a second against main's 104.3 and 94.4 (the installed build's 124.1 and 116.4), the window's mux client thread 3.1 against 131 to 161, its GUI thread 21 and 24 against 34 and 40, its process 42 and 60 against 189 and 227, 12.7 pushes a second against 240; under the lines flood out of view the server's main thread 0.47 and 0.65 µs a line against 8.0 and 8.2 and the client thread 16 and 22 ms a second against 686 and 694, the GUI thread unchanged (209 and 238 against 234 and 247), all of it the hidden tabs' titles, which go at once as decided; with a flooding tab in view the server's main thread 1.8 and 2.2 µs a line against 8.7 and 9.0. What it leaves: the shown tab's pushes (What could go 12) and the titles of tabs out of view, one push each.
   - Reviewed and fixed up (2026-11-08, evening): the word of a hold goes only for output that changes what a push carries (PITFALLS 197, at-rest passes again), a reconnect asks each held pane's push again (PITFALLS 201), the quiet timer is kept across the connection's passes (row 38, PITFALLS 203), and run 12 measured mux-load against main's build with the fix-up: the server's main thread 6.9 and 7.0 ms a second against 108.6 and 125, the window's process 48 and 49 against 220 and 258, a key's echo 10 and 9 ms at the 95th against 13 and 13. What could go 12 was built after it (the second round's stages 4 and 5), and the push after a hold longer than 256 batches on a 50,000-line scrollback was measured in the round's stage 6 (run 15; MUX.md, "What the server holds"): about a millisecond more of the server's main thread, once, and not what a switch waits for.
   - Decided (Lachlan, 2026-11-08, his answers to the review's six questions, asked and answered that day; MUX.md's design bullets have each where it applies). 1a: the switch's wait for a held pane's push stays capped at 50 ms (`SWITCH_WAIT`), counted from the first paint the new view tries; it turned on every round trip measured being 0.3 to 5 ms (the waits since 0.4 to 17, mux-hidden's busy switches 0.97 to 5.2), and on a server that doesn't answer in time (a 50,000-line pane's rewrap, 25 to 30 ms; a reply to a program that stopped reading) costing a switch at most about twice its usual before it draws. 2b: during the wait only the tab's panes hold; the rail, the strip, fades and the rest of the window paint as they would, so their motion never pauses, and a frame may show the new rail over the old screen for the wait, which he chose over holding the whole window (stages 1 to 5 held every paint of it, so the rail's glide and the strip's swap started only when the push came). 3a: a PaneHeld that arrives after the switch's first frame has drawn leaves that screen up for the few ms until the push, as built. 4a: a minimized window still counts as showing its active tab, as built (holding it would save one tab's pushes among 8 to 18, make the restore wait and need the client domain to hear a GUI window's state). 5a: a key, a write or Lua's `send_text` to a tab out of view reaches its program at once, and only the window's copy of the echo waits until the tab is quiet or shown, as built. 6b: a read of a held pane from the window side (Lua's `get_lines_as_text` and `get_cursor_position`, `lbterm cli get-text` through the window's own socket, anything else in the window that reads a pane's lines) asks the server first, so it's always current, rather than reading it as last pushed; built in the second round's stage 3 (below). It turned on Conductor and Earshot reading through the server already (current), and on the GUI thread taking every key and Lua callback, so a read there never waits without a bound: a cli request can await its answer off the GUI thread, Lua's calls are synchronous on it. And his 2a: What could go 12 is built in this round, finishing his 12b of 2026-11-07.
   - 2b built (the second round's stage 2, 2026-11-08): each paint asks at its start whose panes it draws (wezterm-gui's termwindow/frame_wait.rs, `FrameTab`), and while a new view's held pane hasn't had its push the frame draws, in the panes' room, the tab drawn last's panes as they were, the rest as it would; what draws there reads those (PITFALLS 222); the whole-window paint hold went with the window crate's part of it (rows 70 and 71). What it costs (MUX.md, "The switch's first frame"; run 13): the new tab's panes come at the frame after the push, a frame period at least after the switch's first frame, where the held window drew them as the push came (the busy tab's screen about a frame later at the median, the rail and the strip 16 to 30 ms sooner); the strip's words dissolve from the first frame and the panes from the push's; with nothing drawn before the wait to keep, the panes' room shows the window's background until the push.
   - 6b built (the second round's stage 3, 2026-11-08; MUX.md, "What reads a held pane's lines", which lists every reader and what 6b does to each): a read of a held pane from the window side asks its server first and applies the push it brings before it reads. The flow of one read: the reader (Lua's reading pane methods, `cli get-text`'s two requests through the window's own listener, a voice draft's send or edit to a pane by id through the control pipe) asks the pane (`Pane::lbterm_current_now` or `lbterm_current`): an atomic load and nothing more unless the pane is held; held, and the connection up and not late, the GUI thread queues GetPaneRenderChanges to the client thread without waiting (an unbounded channel's `try_send`), the client thread encodes and writes it, the server answers it on its main thread behind the pane's resizes under way with the pane's push (`maybe_push_pane_changes`: the push read and copied as any push is, after a long hold reading every line's seqno of the scrollback once) and then its LivenessResponse, in that order on the one connection; the client thread decodes the push into the backlog and the answer to the waiting reader, reading on past the backlog's limit for it (`Input::awaited`); the reader takes the pane's pushes from the backlog and applies them (`apply_changes_to_surface`, its PaneOutput notice, the hold ended), then reads the window's copy, now the server's. It needs to run only when the copy is behind and the reader needs what the program has (a frame waits for a switch's push itself, and hidden_scan reads the copy it will draw); every step but the ask's queueing and the apply is off the GUI thread. Each read took 0.66, 0.69 and 0.82 ms from the ask to the push applied (perf/held-reads.ps1 on this build, 2026-11-08 23:38, the PC unlocked, one warden GPU job beside another track's test window), the GUI thread waiting all of that in Lua's case; the bound (`HELD_READ_WAIT`, 50 ms) was never reached. On stage 2's build the same script's Lua read an empty screen and `cli get-text` no line where the tab had written 52 and 65, and the draft was refused as the permission prompt showing, which the stand-in had left.
   - Decided by Lachlan on 2026-11-09 (gathered by the second round's fix-up from the stages' reports and the review, asked as his Q12 to Q15 that morning, answered 12a, 13a, 14a and 15a: every one kept as built, as said here). (Q1) When nothing was drawn before a switch's wait (the Conductor's view just closed onto a held tab, a window's first frame on a mux domain, the tab drawn last closed), the panes' room shows the window's background until the push or the 50 ms bound (`FrameTab::Empty`), about a frame when the server answers; the build before 2b held the whole window, so the Conductor's view stayed up instead, and picking a session from the view onto a tab that printed out of view is a common path for him. Keep the background, or keep the view's last frame in the panes' room while `FrameWait::drawn` is None and a held pane waits (not built). (Q2) The scroll strip goes with the kept panes, its picture fading with them, while the window strip's words go with the rail at the switch's first frame, so the strip's words and the panes are out of step for the wait (one frame period here, 16.8 to 17.6 ms); DESIGN.md has them in step. (Q3) With 2b the new tab's screen comes about a frame later than with the whole-window hold when the push is quick (run 13: its panes 9 to 11 ms later in the second set), the rail and strip moving 16 to 30 ms sooner. (Q4) `lbterm cli list` through the window's own socket still gives each held pane's cursor and top row as last pushed: a list covers every pane, and asking would end every hold at each list. (Q5) 6b's "current" covers a held pane's screen, cursor and size; scrollback rows above the screen the window doesn't hold read blank, as on any client pane, and are fetched for a later read, rows a long hold's push named as scrolled away included; reads don't wait for those fetches. (Q6) Input to a pane (a key, a write, a paste, a mouse event) lets its round pushes go unpaced until a frame has read the first push made since, so an echo never waits for a frame: up to a frame of extra pushes a key in a pane that floods (rule 6, PITFALLS 235). (Q7) 6b's reads ask the server only for a held pane: a Lua read or `cli get-text` of a paced pane in view can be up to a frame plus a round trip behind the server's. (Q8) A tab printing out of view that sets its title as it goes (title-storm's stand-in for a working Claude Code session changes it every 960 ms) has its titles go alone and its lines held at the server until it's shown or quiet: the design, which the branch only did from stage 6's fix (PITFALLS 242). (Q9) Under a flood in view the newest line a frame draws is about 6 ms older at the median (20 to 21 ms against main's 14 to 15, at most 47 against 32; run 15), and a flood's last screen comes 16 to 40 ms after it's written against 10 to 25; What could go 12's design said it could.
3. **A line's quads are cached by what the line shows, not where it is.** `LineQuadCacheKey` without `top_pixel_y` and `phys_line_idx`. A line's quads are laid out at row 0 and kept as flat vertices with their hashes, as `render/kept_quads.rs`'s `lbterm_vertices` keeps the rail's. The row's offset is added as they're copied into the layer and folded into each quad's hash, so the compositor's content keys and the upload's dirty runs still see the move.
   - Rows: 85 (the key), 86 (`to_vertices` a quad becomes a copy and an add), 87 for a line that only moved (laid out again, a Box a quad), 91 (dead entries evicted), 93 (most of the heap).
   - Saves, from run 4's scrolling frame of 9.3 to 9.9 ms of the GUI thread: `render_screen_line`'s 3.06 ms down to the one new row's, the 1.0 ms of evictions, about 1.3 of `apply_to`'s 1.75 ms, the heap's 2.2 ms mostly with them. About 5 ms a frame, about 150 ms a second of the GUI thread at 29 frames a second, while the open tab scrolls. perf/mux-client doesn't touch it.
   - Could break, and what Lachlan would notice: anything drawn into a line's quads that depends on its row and isn't in the key would be drawn at another row's (the cursor's and selection's rows are keyed already; links' hover and the trigger recolour keep their own keys); an offset left out of the quads' hashes would leave the pane's visual undrawn after a scroll, an old frame on screen. He'd notice a session streaming in the open tab costing about half the frame it does now. Claude Code's fullscreen view moves its conversation up a region at a time, which on the alternate screen dirties every row of the region with its content one row up, so this applies to it: it does so about once a tool call, by DECSTBM and SU (run 5, below).
   - Size: upstream's `render/pane.rs`, `render/mod.rs` (the key), `render/screen_line.rs`, `quad.rs` (`HeapQuadAllocator`) and `renderstate.rs`'s copy into the layer: a few hundred lines, all on the hot path.
   - Saves for real Claude Code (run 5, his 12a's measurement), which isn't run 4's frame: most of its frames rewrite a row in place, which today's key keeps too. Of run 5's 265 frames, taken at most one a 16.7 ms as the window draws them, 240 changed rows in place (its spinner, a token count: one row each; with the spinner alone 0.4 rows a frame laid out again, its glyphs repeating), 12 changed nothing on screen, and 13 moved the conversation up by DECSTBM and SU: one for each of the 10 tool calls (4 or 5 rows), the 2 prompts typed after an answer (6) and the tools' answer (12), where the long answer's landing rewrote 67 rows in place: in the 12 where a prompt or a tool call joined it, 54 to 84 rows changed, 39 to 60 of them moved, 12 to 19 blank and 2 to 4 new, and in the one where the answer joined, 64 changed, 20 moved, 15 shown at their own row before and 13 new. Rows laid out again over the run, by the model's shape hashes with a history of the cache's 4,096 lines (`mux/tests/lbterm_pty_frames.rs`): 1,225 with today's key against 343 with one by content; in the turn of 10 tool calls 714 against 84 in 5.1 s; with the spinner alone 18 against 18; while the answer streamed 106 against 106. At run 4's cost of a row laid out (34 µs of `render_screen_line`, 11 of its eviction), the key's part saves about 2.5 to 3 ms on each frame that moves the conversation: about 5 ms a second while tool calls run, nothing while only the spinner or a token count changes. The copy's part (row 86's quads copied as kept vertices rather than built from boxed quads) runs in every frame of the open tab: about 1.3 ms of each by run 4, 7 to 19 ms a second at Claude Code's 5.5 to 14 frames a second. So about 7 to 24 ms a second of the GUI thread while a Claude Code session works in the open tab, most of it the copy, against the 150 run 4's frame suggested; estimated from run 4's costs a row, whose rows were coloured lines of about 90 characters (Claude Code's are shorter and many blank, so likely less), until the GUI's cost of Claude Code's own frames is measured (Not traced yet). And the stand-in API's: against it Claude Code drew no streamed text until the answer ended (only a token count), so the streaming span's 106 against 106 is the stand-in's. If the real API's stream is drawn as it arrives, each chunk drawn grows the conversation and moves it up by SU, the case where the key by content saves most (2.5 to 3 ms a frame that moves), so this figure would come out low for a streaming turn; one real turn recorded with LBTERM_PTY_RECORD would say (Lachlan's call, since it reaches the real API; Not traced yet).
   - Decided (Lachlan, 2026-11-04, his 13a and 12a): the first of these to be built, as a track, after one measurement of real Claude Code's output (how it writes, how often its frames are a scroll, whether DECSET 2026 gets through ConPTY), which sets this one's size and proposal 4's gap.
   - Built (perf/line-quads-by-content, 2026-11-04), as written, rows 83 to 87, 91 and 93 as they now read; the key made in `render/line_key.rs`'s `LineFrame::line` from a line's inputs and its row, the row's pixel y beside it. The key is what the line shows; the list under segment 4's table says where each thing a line's quads take from its row goes (the key, the copy, or drawn apart).
